This work was supported by the National Key Research and Development Program of China (2023YFC3208102, 2022YFF0606905), and the Ningbo science and technology plan project (2023Z057).\n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n## Graphical abstract\n\nImage: Image, graphical abstract\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (273KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\n  * Previous article in issue\n  * Next article in issue\n\n## Keywords\n\nExplainability\n\nReal-time\n\nDemand forecasting\n\nAttention mechanisms\n\nGraph neural network\n\nWater distribution system\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. \nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.\n\n### 2.1. Problem formulation\n\nBefore discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph \\mathcal{G}=\\left(\\mathcal{V},\\mathcal{E},\\mathcal{W}\\right). Here, \\mathcal{V} is the set of I+{I}^{*} vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. \\mathcal{E} is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. \\mathcal{W}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times \\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)} is the weighted adjacency matrix. \nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\n\nImage: Fig 1\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (318KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\n\n(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} with the weighted adjacency matrix {\\mathcal{W}}_{{v}_{i}}, the past CHPS of sensor node {v}_{i} is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5)){\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}={\\mathcal{W}}_{{v}_{i}}·{c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}where l is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(0\\right)} is an initialized learnable parameter.\nIn the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\n\n### 3.2. Model settings\n\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\n\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\n\n### 3.3. Evaluation metric\n\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:(13)MAE=\\frac{1}{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times {J}^{*}}\\sum _{i=1}^{I+{I}^{*}}\\sum _{j=1}^{{J}^{*}}\\left|{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}-{\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}\\right|(14)MAPE=\\frac{1}{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times {J}^{*}}\\sum _{i=1}^{I+{I}^{*}}\\sum _{j=1}^{{J}^{*}}\\frac{\\left|{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}-{\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}\\right|}{{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}}where {h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}} is the prediction and {\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}} is the simulated measurement of vector {v}_{i} at time step {t}_{j}.\n\n## 4. Results and discussion\n\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\n\n### 4.1. Forecasting results\n\n#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\n\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. #### 4.1.2. Temporal forecasting accuracy\n\nTo further analyze the fluctuations in prediction accuracy over time, Fig. 4 presents the MAPE variation for 328 non-overlapping test batches in a time series format. This visualization enhances our understanding of the model’s dynamic performance and highlights the superior long-term effectiveness of the AGN method. In each test batch, the MAPE of AGN remains impressively below 5 %, demonstrating significant advantages in both prediction accuracy and stability over the GGNN and GHR+GRU methods.\n\nImage: Fig 4\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (373KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 4. Comparison of forecasting models in temporal direction.\n\nNotably, the AGN model maintains stable and low MAPE levels even after more than a month of continuous testing. This remarkable performance is a strong testament to its robustness in time-series prediction. This stability is also crucial for practical applications, allowing the model to operate effectively long-term and significantly reduce dependence on computational resources.\n\nDespite these limitations, the results of the demand prediction displayed along the time axis clearly show the long-term robustness of the AGN model in practical deployment environments. Notably, the model was trained on a consumer-grade GPU platform (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 Ti), requiring approximately 3 h in total. Leveraging the computational efficiency inherent to deep learning methods, the model achieves an inference time of <1 s per run, sufficiently satisfies the requirement for real-time prediction. These findings not only enhance our confidence in the model’s performance but also provides strong support and insights for its future application in broader and more complex scenarios. Subsequent sections analyze prediction accuracy variations across time steps and regions.\n\n#### 4.1.3. Accuracy analysis at varying time steps\n\nTo thoroughly investigate the prediction accuracy of the proposed AGN model across three different elevation regions with varying prediction steps, we tested four different prediction steps and presented the MAE and MAPE for the low, medium, and high regions separately (Table 1). This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. ## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\nThis study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.\n\n## CRediT authorship contribution statement\n\nZekun Zou: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Software, Methodology, Investigation, Formal analysis, Conceptualization. METHOD TARGET autoencoders
p 5 re z ¼ E(x). During training, AEs aim to minimise the difference between the input data (x) and the reconstructed data ^x. MSE as a loss function, is used to train the AE model. By minimising this error, the AE learns a compressed representation that captures the most salient features of the input data. Hence, any considerable deviations in the reconstruction errors can be attributed to the onset of anomalous events. AEs have been widely used in classiﬁcation and regression tasks across several domains. For example, AEs have been used for monitoring pipeline leakage (Wang et al. 2020) and for monitoring spacecraft sensor data (Sakurada & Yairi 2014). In this study, a simple AE is trained in semi-supervised fashion for the leak detection task. Long short-term memory autoencoder LSTM are a t
p 6 tion at latent space z is sampled via inferential learning and fed to the decoder. The decoder then reconstructs the sampled information at latent space to the original dimension of input data x. VAEs aim to minimise the reconstruction error, similar to other AEs. Additionally, they also optimise the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between the learned distribution and the standard normal distribution (prior), ensuring that the latent space distribution remains close to a unit Gaussian. With the inferential learning abilities, VAEs can also be used as a generative model. For example, to generate synthetic data that resembles real sensor measurements, which is beneﬁcial for enhancing the generalisation capabilities of predictive models in WDNs. VAEs have also been used for anomaly detection

As test scenarios, 33 different datasets are generated for each different single leak scen- ario. The leak locations, their magnitudes, start times and end times are, however, the same as in the original BattLeDIM competition.
